     OSI Model (Open Systems Interconnection Model)
     The OSI model is a conceptual framework used to understand and implement standard protocols in
     network communications. It divides the communication process into seven layers, each with specific
     functions.
     OSI wdlD;sh hkq cd, ikaksfõokfha iïu; kshudj,sh f;areï .ekSug iy l%shd;aul lsÍug Ndú;d
     lrk ixl,amSh rduqjls' th ikaksfõok l%shdj,sh ia:r y;lg fnod we;" fïjdg tla tla úfYaIs;
     ld¾hhka we;'

     Physical Layer (Layer 1)
     fN!;sl ia;rh ^1 jk ia;rh&
     Transmits raw bit stream over a physical medium.
     fN!;sl udOHhla yryd raw bit stream iïfm%aIKh lrhs'

     Data Link Layer (Layer 2)
     o;a; iïnkaOl ia;rh ^2 jk ia;rh&
     Provides node-to-node data transfer and handles error correction from the physical layer.
     node-to-node o;a; yqjudrej imhk w;r fN!;sl ia:rfhka fodaI ksjerÈ lsÍu yiqrejhs'

     Network Layer (Layer 3)
     cd, ia;rh ^3 jk ia;rh&
     Determines the best physical path for data to reach its destination.
     o;a; tys .ukdka;hg <Õd ùug fyd|u fN!;sl ud¾.h ;SrKh lrhs'

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                -24-                                           #ictfromabc

     Transport Layer (Layer 4)
     m%jdyk ia;rh ^4 jk ia;rh&
     Ensures complete data transfer with error recovery and flow control.
     fodaI m%;sidOkh iy m%jdy md,kh iuÕ iïmQ¾K o;a; yqjudrej iy;sl lrhs'

     Session Layer (Layer 5)
     ieis ia;rh ^5 jk ia;rh&
     Manages sessions or connections between applications.
     fhÿï w;r ieis fyda iïnkaO;d l<ukdlrKh lrhs'

     Presentation Layer (Layer 6)
     iu¾mK ia;rh ^6 jk ia;rh&
     Translates data between the application layer and the network format, including encryption and
     compression.
     ixfla;kh iy iïmSvkh we;=¿j fhÿï ia:rh iy cd, wdlD;sh w;r o;a; mßj¾;kh lrhs'

     Application Layer (Layer 7)
     fhÿï ia;rh ^7 jk ia;rh&
     Provides network services to end-users.
     wjidk mßYS,lhskag cd, fiajd imhhs'

     TCP/IP Model (Transmission Control Protocol/Internet Protocol Model)

     The TCP/IP model is a simplified, more practical version of the OSI model. It consists of four layers that
     correspond closely to certain layers of the OSI model.
     TCP/IP wdlD;sh hkq OSI wdlD;sfha ir, l<" jvd;a m%dfhda.sl wkqjdohls' th OSI wdlD;sfha we;eï
     ia:rj,g iómj wkqrEm jk ia:r y;rlska iukaú; fõ'

     Network Access Layer (Link Layer)
     cd, m%fõY ia;rh ^ine¢ ia;rh&
     Corresponds to the OSI's physical and data link layers. Handles the physical transmission of data.
     OSI ys fN!;sl iy o;a; iïnkaOl ia:rj,g wkqrEm fõ' o;a; fN!;sl iïfm%aIKh yiqrejhs'

     Internet Layer
     wka;¾cd, ia;rh
     Corresponds to the OSI's network layer. Handles logical addressing and routing.
     OSI ys cd, ia:rhg wkqrEm fõ' ;d¾lsl ,smsk iy ud¾..; lsÍu yiqrejhs'

     Transport Layer
     m%jdyk ia;rh
     Corresponds to the OSI's transport layer. Ensures reliable data transfer between host systems.
     OSI ys m%jdyk ia;rhg wkqrEm fõ' Odrl moaO;s w;r úYajikSh o;a; yqjudrej iy;sl lrhs'

     Application Layer
     fhÿï ia;rh
     Corresponds to the OSI's session, presentation, and application layers. Provides application services to the
     user.
     OSI ys ieis" bÈßm;a lsÍu iy fhÿï ia:r j,g wkqrEm fõ' mßYS,lhdg fhÿï fiajd imhhs'

     There are two types of encryption techniques used namely, symmetric key encryption and asymmetric key
     encryption. In ……P…… key encryption, the same key is used for encrypting and decrypting the
     information. In this scheme, to exchange information, users must share a ……Q…… key

     Encryption is a process used to convert plain text or data into an unreadable format, known as ciphertext,
     to protect it from unauthorized access. This transformation is achieved using algorithms and encryption
     keys. The primary purpose of encryption is to ensure the confidentiality and integrity of data.
     ixfla;kh hkq ir, fm< fyda o;a; lshúh fkdyels wdlD;shla njg mßj¾;kh lsÍug Ndú;d lrk

     Types of Encryptions
     ixfla;dxlk j¾.

    I. Symmetric Key
       iuñ;sl h;=r

         Encryption ixfla;kh
         Uses the same key for both encryption and decryption.
         ixfla;kh iy úfla;kh hk folgu tlu h;=r Ndú;d lrhs'

         Pros: Faster and efficient for processing large amounts of data.
         jdis( úYd, o;a; m%udKhla ieliSu i|yd fõ.j;a yd ld¾hlaIu fõ'

         Cons: Key distribution can be challenging, as the same key must be securely shared among users.
         wjdis( h;=re fnod yeÍu wNsfhda.d;aul úh yel" tlu h;=r mßYS,lhka w;r wdrlaIs;j
         fnod.; hq;=h'
         Examples: AES (Advanced Encryption Standard), DES (Data Encryption Standard).

    II. Asymmetric Key
        wiuñ;sl h;=r

         Encryption ixfla;kh

         Uses a pair of keys – a public key for encryption and a private key for decryption.
         ixfla;kh i|yd fmdÿ h;=rla iy úfla;kh i|yd mqoa.,sl h;=rla f,i h;=re hq.,hla Ndú;d
         lrhs'
         Pros: Enhanced security as the private key is never shared; only the public key is distributed.
         jdis( mqoa.,sl h;=r lsis úfgl fnod fkd.kakd neúka wdrlaIdj by< hs' fnod yßkq ,nkafka
         fmdÿ h;=rla muKs'
         Cons: Slower than symmetric encryption and requires more computational resources.
         wjdis( iuñ;sl ixfla;khg jvd ukao.dó jk w;r jeä mß.Kl iïm;a wjYH fõ'
         Examples: RSA (Rivest-Shamir-Adleman), ECC (Elliptic Curve Cryptography).

     •   Caching ksys; lsÍu

  (ii) What is the functionality of a Dynamic Host Configuration Protocol (DHCP) server?
       .;sl Odrl md,l kshudj,s (DHCP) fiajdodhlhl l%shdldß;ajh l=ula o?

     A Dynamic Host Configuration Protocol (DHCP) server plays a crucial role in managing and automating
     the assignment of IP addresses and other network configuration details to devices on a network.

     •   Agent / tacka;

     •   Multi-Agent System / nyqtAcka; moaO;sh
         A multi-agent system (MAS) depicted in the image refers to a network of autonomous agents
         collaborating to monitor and manage a greenhouse. Each agent has specific roles, Agent 1 handles
         user interactions and data access, triggering actions for Agent 2. Agent 2 processes captured images,
         detects anomalies, and can prompt the Search Agent for additional online information. The Search
         Agent retrieves relevant data, updates the database, and alerts Agent 2 if necessary. This setup
         optimizes greenhouse management by distributing tasks among specialized agents.
         rEmfha oelafjk nyqtacka; moaO;sh (MAS) hkq yß;d.drhla ksÍlaIKh lsÍug iy l<ukdlrKh
         lsÍug iyfhda.Sj lghq;= lrk iajhxl%Sh tacka;hskaf.a cd,hls' iEu tacka;jgfhl==gu ksYaÑ;

  (iii) After long-term use of the system, it was decided to remove Search Agent assuming that the database

        moaO;sfha È.=ld,Sk Ndú;hlska miq" o;a; iuqodh wjYH ish¨u f;dr;=rej,ska iukaú; hehs is;d"

8. (a) What is the output of the following python script if 1002 is given as the input?

     A = int(input("Enter a number:"))

           C = A % 10
           B = B + C

     print(B)

     There are 2 types of loops in python:

     1. While loops
     2. For loops

     1. While loops

         While loops execute the loop as long as the condition of the loop is true or until the loop hits a break
         statement
         Æmfha ;;a;ajh i;H jk ;dla fyda Æmhg break m%ldYh yuqjk f;la While Æmh l%shd;aul fõ'
         In while loops we should manually add a control variable / condition to stop infinite looping
         Æm ;=<§ wkka; Æm ùu keje;aùug md,k úp,Hhla $ fldkafoaishla tl;= l< hq;=h'
         Structure of a while loop;
         While Æmfha jHqyh;

             i = 0 #control variable
                while (Condition):
                   #process of the code goes here
                   i = i+1 #increament/decreament

     2. For loops:
         For loops loop until the given range is met, it does not loop according to a condition but we can
         control the loop according to a condition using if statements and break statements inside the loop
         For Æm" § we;s mrdih imqrd,k ;=re mqkrdj¾;kh fõ" th fldkafoaishla wkqj mqkrdj¾;kh
         fkdfõ" kuq;a wmg Æmh ;=< we;s if m%ldY iy break m%ldY Ndú;d lr fldkafoaishlg wkqj
         Æmh md,kh l< yelsh'
         For loops can do the increment by itself
         For Æm j,g jeä lsÍu ;ksju l< yelsh

         Mainly we use the range() function with for loops.

     Structure of a for loop:
              for i in (range):
                    #process

     Here i is the control variable, we don’t need to define it prior to the loop.
     Each time the loop executes, the control variable (i) changes according to the range, string, list or tuple
     given initially.
     fuys i hkq md,k úp,Hh fõ" wmg th Æmhg fmr w¾: oelaùug wjYH fkdfõ'

     fyda tuple wkqj fjkia fõ'

     Here the while loop first checks whether A is larger than zero as the condition if so the loop will be
     executed.
     fuys§ while Æmh m<uqj mÍlaId lrkafka A Y=kHhg jvd úYd,o hkakhs' tfia Æmh l%shd;aul fõ'

     Inside the loop it does modulus division on A by 10 and stores it in the variable C
     Æmh ;=< th A u; b;sß fn§u 10 ka isÿ lr th C úp,Hfha .nvd lrhs'

     Modulus division returns the remainder of a division as the answer
     b;sß fn§fï§ ms<s;=r f,i fn§ul b;sßh ,nd fohs

              At first A = 1002
              uq,§ A = 1002

     When A%10 is executed it divides 1002 by 10 and returns the remainder as the answer. This means it
     stores 2 in the variable C in the first iteration.
     A%10 l%shd;aul l< úg th 1002" ka 10ka fnok w;r b;sßh ms<s;=r f,i ,nd fohs' fuhska
     woyia lrkafka th m<uq mqkrdj¾;kfha§ C úp,Hfha 2 .nvd lrk njhs'

     Then B + C is stored inside B
     túg B + C .nvd jkafka B ;=<h'

     And A is subjected to integer division by 10 and store in A itself
     ;jo A" 10 ka ksÅ, fn§ug hg;a jk w;r A ;=<u .nvd lrhs'
     This means A is divided by 10 but only the integer part is Considered
     fuhska woyia lrkafka A" 10 ka fn¥ kuq;a mQ¾K ixLHd fldgi muKla ie,fla'

     Next the loop iterate for the 2nd time if we trace the loop it gives us the below table
     B<Õg Æmh 2 fjks j;djg;a mqkrdj¾;kh lrhs' wms Æmh ie,l=fjd;a wmg my; j.=j ,nd .;
     yelsh'

  (ii) What would be the modification required to the B = B + C code line of the above code if the reverse
       of a given positive number is to be printed?
       foke ,oe Oke ixLHdjle m%;sj¾;khe (reverse) m%;sodkhe lr .ekSug" by;e mhs;ka fla;fhe
       B = B + C fma<sh flf,i fjkia úh hq;= o?

     ( Example: if the input is 1234, the output should be 4321 )
     ( Wod: wdodkh 1234 jQ úg" m%;sodkh 4321 úh hq;= h )

     Answer :- B = B*10 + C

     To solve this problem lets assume the value we input is 4567 and trace the loop for the given code. This
     will give us the following table;
     fuu .eg¿j úi£u i|yd wms wdodkh lrk w.h 4567 hehs Wml,amkh lr ,nd § we;s fla;h
     i|yd Æmh fidhd .ksuq' fuh wmg my; j.=fjka ,nd fokq we;'

       Repetition      C         B          A
          1            7         7         456
          2            6         15         45
          3            5         20          4
          4            4         24          0

     If we clearly observe this table, we can see the reverse of the given number is stored in C from top to
     bottom in the table
     wms fuu j.=j meyeÈ,sj ksÍlaIKh l<fyd;a" ,nd § we;s wxlfha m%;sf,dau j.=fõ by< isg
     my<g C j,ska .nvd lr we;s nj wmg oel.; yelsh'

     To get the B to output the reverse order we should make the number 7654 which means 7 should be in
     1000’s place 6 should be 100’s place should be in 10’s place and 4 should be in units place
     m%;sf,dau wkqms<sfj,g B ,nd .ekSu i|yd wms 7654 wxlh iEÈh hq;=h" tkï 7" 1000 ia:dkfhao
     6, 100 ia:dkfhao 5" 10 ia:dkfhao iy 4" 1 ia:dkfhao ;sìh hq;=h'

     This means to make 7654 we should add 7000 + 600 + 50 + 4
     fuhska woyia lrkafka 7654 iE§ug wms 7000 + 600 + 50 + 4 tl;= l< hq;= njhs'

     So if we multiply the B variable by 10 before adding the C value we can smoothly achieve the objective,
     b;ska wms C w.h tl;= lsÍug fmr B úp,Hh 10 ka .=K l<fyd;a wmg wruqK idlaId;a lr.;
     yelsh"

     B = B*10 + C

     What this does is that it will multiply the value in be by 10 in other words shift the value that is already
     inside B by a place of 10
     fuh isÿ lrkafka th B ys w.h 10 ka .=K lsÍu fjk;a jpk j,ska lsjfyd;a B ;=< oekgu;a
     we;s w.h 10 ia:dkhlska udre lsÍuhs'

     Now if we trace the loop for the change we did the table should look like this
     oeka wms lrmq fjkig wod< Æmh msgm;a l<fyd;a j.=j my; wdldrfhka úh hq;=h'

       Repetition     C        B         A
          1           7         7       456
          2           6        76        45
          3           5       765         4
          4           4       7654        0
     Now if we print B at the end it prints the reverse order of the number we initial give as the input.
     oeka wms wjidkfha B uqøKh l<fyd;a th wm uq,ska fok wxlfha m%;sf,dau wkqms<sfj, uqøKh
     lrhs'

    parameters. The function should return True or False to indicate whether the student is allowed to
    bring the food item to the party or not.

    Note: You can assume that student name and food item name are always lowercase strings and
          have at least two letters. There may be hyphens and spaces in the names, but these will not

    igyk: YsIHhdf.a ku iy wdydr j¾.fha ku ieuõgu l=vd wl=re ^lemsg,a fkdjk&" iys; wlaIr

    Solving this problem is very easy if we have a good knowledge about string slicing, lets revise.
    string slicing .ek wmg fyd| oekqula ;sfí kï fuu .eg¿j úi£u myiq fõ" .eg¨j i,ld n,uq.

    Indexes of a string
    string tll o¾Yl

    Let’s take the string “Percy” as an example the indexes of this string will be as follows
    WodyrKhla f,i “Percy” string tl .ksuq' fuu string tflys o¾Yl my; mßÈ jkq we;'

    We have two methods of indexing in python positive and negative.
    mhs;ka Ok iy iDK o¾Yl lsÍfï l%u folla wmg ;sfí'

        0      1      2       3      4
       P       e      r       c      y
       -5     -4     -3      -2     -1

    Now we can see that we can call the first letter of the string by the index of zero and no matter how long
    the string is we can call the last letter by using the negative index -1 understanding this solves the
    problem itself
    oeka wmsg tflys uq,a wl=r Y=kH o¾Ylfhka ye¢kaúh yels w;r tAh fldmuK È. jqk;a wjidk
    wl=rg iDK o¾Ylh -1 fhdod l;d lsÍug yels nj f;areï .ekSfuka .eg¨j úi¢h yelsh'

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                 -22-                                           #ictfromabc

    Now we should just check whether the first letter (First index ) of the name is equal to the first letter of
    the food item and the last letter of the name is equal to the last letter of the food item
    kfï uq,a wl=r ^m<uq o¾Ylh& wdydr øjHfha uq,a wl=rg iudko" kfï wjidk wl=r wdydr øjHfha
    wjidk wl=rg iudko hkak oeka wm úiska mÍlaId l< hq;=h'

    def Party(name,fditem): #here we define a function where it takes 2 arguments
     a = name[0] #here we store the first letter of the name in a variable
     b = name [-1] #here we store the last letter of the name in b variable
     c = fditem[0] #here we store the first letter of the food item in c
     d = fditem[-1] #here we store the last letter of the food item in d

     if a == c and b == d: #here we check the condition for bringing food
           return True
     else:
           return False

    we can further simplify this without using many variables instead directly checking the condition;
    ;;ajh fl,skau mÍlaId lsÍu fjkqjg fndfyda úp,Hhka Ndú;d fkdlr wmg fuh ;jÿrg;a ir,
    l< yelsh;

    def Party(name, fditem):
     if name[0] == fditemp[0] and name[-1] == fditem[-1]:
           return True
     else:
           return False

    If we dive deeper into the comparison operator (==);
    wms iei£fï fufyhjkh (==) ms<sn| jeäÿr fiùfuka;

    This operator checks whether the left side is equal to the right side and the operator itself returns whether
    the condition is true or false

    wi;Ho hkak fufyhjkh úiskau ,nd fohs'

    This means we can further take away the if condition;
    fuhska woyia lrkafka wmg if fldkafoaish ;jÿrg;a bj;a l< yels njhs;

    def Party(name, fditem):
     return name[0] == fditem[0] and name[-1] == fditem[-1]

     by this if it is true as we initially have the factorial variable set to 1 if n is 1 this condition will directly

     iDcqju display factorial wvx.= G fldgqj fj; fhduq lrhs'

     Next in the E and F boxes must be used to calculate the factorial, here note that if the input is 5 the
     factorial should be 5 x 4 x 3 x 2 x 1 this is the same as 1 x 5 x 4 x 3 x 2

     So we can use the following algorithm in the E box factorial = factorial x n and in the F box we can do

     A student uses the following data to register for subjects. Each subject is represented by its subject

     register for more than one subject during a semester. Each student has a first name (studentFname),
     a last name (studentLname) and a unique student number (studentid). Student, age (age) is required

     subject_offering is assigned to a single teacher who is represented by a teacherld, name of the teacher

  (i) Write down the output of the following SQL statement:
      my; SQL m%ldYfha m%;sodkh ,shd olajkak'

     SELECT Employee.FullName, Employee.Salary
     FROM Employee, Assign_Project
     WHERE Assign Project Employeeld = Employee Employeeld;

     The two tables detail employee information and project assignments. The "Employee" table lists
     employees with their ID, name, date of birth, and salary, where EmployeeId is the primary key. The
     "Assign_Project" table shows which projects are assigned to which employees, identified by their
     employee ID (foreign key), and includes the project ID (primary key), project name and description.
     j.= fol fiajl f;dr;=re iy jHdmD;s mejreï úia;r lrhs' "Employee" j.=fõ fiajlhska Tjqkaf.a
     ye÷kqïm;" ku" Wmka Èkh iy jegqm iuÕ ,ehsia;=.; lrhs" tys§ EmployeeId m%d:ñl h;=r fõ'
     "Assign_Project" j.=j uÕska Tjqkaf.a fiajl ye÷kqïm; ^wd.ka;=l h;=r& u.ska y÷kdf.k" jHdmD;s
     ye÷kqïm; ^m%d:ñl h;=r&" jHdmD;s ku iy úia;rh we;=<;a jk fiajlhskag mjrk ,o jHdmD;s
     fmkajhs'

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                 -31-                                       #ictfromabc

     SELECT Employee.FullName, Employee.Salary
     SELECT:
     This clause specifies the columns you want to retrieve from the tables.
     fuu j.ka;sh Tng j.= j,ska ,nd .ekSug wjYH ;Sre kshu lrhs'
     Employee.FullName, Employee.Salary:
     These are the columns selected for output. The Employee. prefix indicates that these columns are from the
     "Employee" table.
     fïjd m%;sodkh i|yd f;dard.;a ;Sre fõ' Employee. Wmi¾.h fmkakqï lrkafka fuu ;Sre "Employee"
     j.=fjka njhs'

     FROM Employee, Assign_Project
     FROM:
     This clause specifies the tables from which to retrieve the data.
     fuu j.ka;sh o;a; ,nd .ekSug j.= kshu lrhs'
     Employee, Assign_Project:
     These are the tables involved in the query.
     fuu úuiqug iïnkaO j.= fõ'

     WHERE Assign_Project.EmployeeId = Employee.EmployeeId;
     WHERE:
     This clause specifies the condition for filtering the rows.
     fuu j.ka;sh fma<s fmÍu ^filter lsÍu& i|yd fldkafoaish kshu lrhs'
     Assign_Project.EmployeeId = Employee.EmployeeId:
     This condition matches rows from the "Assign_Project" table with rows from the "Employee" table where
     the EmployeeId values are equal. This joins the two tables on the EmployeeId column.
     fuu fldkafoaish "Assign_Project" j.=fõ we;s fma<s yd EmployeeId w.hka iudk jk "Employee"
     j.=fõ we;s fma<s iuÕ .e,fma' fuh EmployeeId ;Srej u; j.= fol iïnkaO lrhs'

     So, this query basically retrieves the names and salaries of employees who have been assigned to projects
     by matching their EmployeeId in the "Assign_Project" table with their EmployeeId in "Employee" table.
     tneúka" fuu úuiqu uQ,sl jYfhka "Assign_Project" j.=fõ we;s Tjqkaf.a EmployeeId iy "Employee"
     j.=fõ we;s EmployeeId iuÕ .e,mSfuka jHdmD;s i|yd wkqhqla; lr we;s fiajlhskaf.a kï iy
     jegqma ,nd .kS'

     Output m%;sodkh :-
     +---------------+-----------+
     | FullName      | Salary    |
     +---------------+-----------+
     | Upul Fernando | 44000.00 |
     | Sunil Perera | 115000.00 |
     | Kamala Gamage | 52000.00 |
     | Upul Fernando | 44000.00 |
     | Saman Perera | 140000.00 |
     | Kamala Gamage | 52000.00 |
     +---------------+-----------+

     Here, repeated data occurs because each row in the "Assign_Project" table that matches a row in the
     "Employee" table produces a result. Since employees can be assigned to multiple projects, each
     assignment will produce a row in the result set.

     The statement in SQL which is used to retrieve data from tables in a database is the SELECT statement.
     It typically follows the following format:
     o;a; iuqodhl j.= j,ska o;a; ,nd .ekSug Ndú;d lrk SQL ys m%ldYh SELECT m%ldYhhs' th
     idudkHfhka my; wdlD;sh wkq.ukh lrhs(

     SELECT column_name1, column_name2, ……
     FROM table_name1, table_name2, ……
     WHERE condition;

     JOIN Clauses in SQL
     SQL ys JOIN j.ka;s

     In SQL, joins are used to combine rows from two or more tables based on a related column between
     them. Here are explanations of the common types of joins:
     SQL ys" tAjd w;r iïnkaê; ;Srejla u; mokïj j.= follska fyda jeä .Kklska fma<s tAldnoaO
     lsÍug iïnkaO lsÍï Ndú;d lrhs' fmdÿ iïnkaOl j¾. ms<sn| meyeÈ,s lsÍï fukak(

     1. INNER JOIN
         An INNER JOIN returns only the rows that have matching values in both tables.
         INNER JOIN tlla ,nd fokafka j.= foflysu .e,fmk w.hka we;s fma<s muKs'

         SELECT columns
         FROM table1
         INNER JOIN table2
         ON table1.column = table2.column;

     2. LEFT JOIN (or LEFT OUTER JOIN)
         A LEFT JOIN returns all the rows from the left table and the matched rows from the right table. If
         no match is found, NULL values are returned for columns from the right table.
         LEFT JOIN tlla jï j.=fjka ish¨u fma<s iy ol=Kq j.=fjka .e,fmk fma<s wdmiq ,nd fohs'
         .e<mSula fidhd.; fkdyels kï" ol=Kq j.=fjka ;Sre i|yd NULL w.hka wdmiq ,nd fokq we;'
         SELECT columns
         FROM table1
         LEFT JOIN table2
         ON table1.column = table2.column;

     3. RIGHT JOIN (or RIGHT OUTER JOIN)
         A RIGHT JOIN returns all the rows from the right table and the matched rows from the left table. If
         no match is found, NULL values are returned for columns from the left table.
         RIGHT JOIN tlla ol=Kq j.=fõ we;s ish¨u fma<s iy jï j.=fjka .e,fmk fma<s ,nd fohs'
         .e<mSula fidhd.; fkdyels kï" jï j.=fjka ;Sre i|yd NULL w.hka ,nd fokq we;'
         SELECT columns
         FROM table1
         RIGHT JOIN table2
         ON table1.column = table2.column;

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                  -33-                                           #ictfromabc

     4. FULL JOIN (or FULL OUTER JOIN)
           A FULL JOIN returns all rows when there is a match in either the left table or the right table. If there
           is no match, the result is NULL on the side where there is no match.
           FULL JOIN tlla jï j.=fõ fyda ol=Kq j.=fõ .e<mSula we;s úg ish¨u fma<s ,nd fohs'
           .e<mSula fkdue;s kï" m%;sM,h NULL jkafka .e<mSula ke;s me;af;a h'
           SELECT columns
           FROM table1
           FULL JOIN table2
           ON table1.column = table2.column;

  (iii) In which normal form does the Assign_Project table exist?
        Assign_Project j.=j l=uk m%u; wjia:dfjys mj;S o?

         Multivalued Columns ^nyq w.h l< ;Sre&             Yes     No     No     No

     To determine the normal form of the Assign_Project table, we need to follow a step by step process.
     Assign_Project j.=fõ m%u; wjia:dj ;SrKh lsÍu i|yd" mshjfrka mshjr l%shdj,shla wkq.ukh
     l< hq;=h'

     Step 1: Ensure First Normal Form (1NF)

     •     In 1NF, table columns should contain indivisible values. There cannot be any multivalued columns.

     Therefore, the table Assign_Project meets the 1NF criteria.
     tneúka" Assign_Project j.=j 1NF ks¾Kdhl imqrd,hs'

     Step 2: Ensure Second Normal Form (2NF)
     mshjr 2: fojk m%u; wjia:dj ^2NF& iy;sl lrkak
     •   2NF requires that the table is in 1NF.
         2NF i|yd j.=j 1NF ys ;sîu wjYH fõ'
     •   All non-key attributes should fully functionally dependent on the entire primary key.
         ish¨u h;=re fkdjk .=Kdx. iïmQ¾K m%d:ñl h;=r u; iïmQ¾Kfhkau l%shdldÍj r|d mej;sh hq;=h'

     Let's assume the primary key is a composite key of ProjectId and EmployeeId.
     m%d:ñl h;=r ProjectId iy EmployeeId ys ixhqla; h;=rla hehs Wml,amkh lruq'
     Identify functional dependencies:
     l%shdldÍ mrdh;a;;d y÷kd .kak(
     •   PName - ProjectId
     •   Description - ProjectId

     Since PName and Description depend only on ProjectId and not on the entire composite key, this table
     has partial dependencies.
     PName iy Description, ProjectId u; muKla r|d mj;sk w;r iïmQ¾K ixhqla; h;=r u; r|d
     fkdmj;sk neúka" fuu j.=jg wdxYsl mrdh;a;;d we;'
     Therefore, the table Assign_Project is not in 2NF due to partial dependencies.
     tneúka" wdxYsl mrdh;a;;d fya;=fjka Assign_Project j.=j 2NF ys fkdue;'

     Step 3: Ensure Third Normal Form (3NF)
     mshjr 3 ( f;jk m%u; wjia:dj ^3NF& iy;sl lrkak
     •   3NF requires that the table is in 2NF.
         3NF i|yd j.=j 2NF ys ;sîu wjYH fõ'
     •   Non-key attributes should not depend on other non-key attributes.
         m%Odk fkdjk .=Kdx. fjk;a m%Odk fkdjk .=Kdx. u; r|d fkdisáh hq;=h'

     Since the table is not in 2NF, it cannot be in 3NF.
     j.=j 2NF ys fkdue;s neúka th 3NF ys ;sìh fkdyel'
     Therefore, Assign_Project table is in 1NF.

     Inconsistent Data
     fkd.e,fmk o;a;(

     updating the PName or Description for only one EmployeeId does not propagate the changes to all rows
     related to the same project.

     lrhs'

 (c) Assume that we have a computer that can use 16-bit virtual addresses from O up to 64 K. Assume

    úYd,;ajh 32 KB jQ l%uf,aLhla mßYS,lhl= úiska fuu mß.Klfha Odjkh lrhs' tla;rd wjia:djl§
    tu l%shdhkfha msgq j.=fõ (page table) f;dard.;a lafIa;% lsysmhla my; mßÈ fõ'

          Page       Frame        Present/
         number      number        absent
           0           011           1
           1           101           1
           2           000           0
           3           000           0
           4           000           0
           5           000           0
           6           000           0
           7           000           0

     Notes :
     •    The frame number is indicated in binary.
          rduq wxlh oaúuh f,i olajd we;.

     •    The virtual addresses on page 0 are from 0 to 4095 and on page 1 are from 4096 to,8191 and so
          on.
          0 msgqfõ w;:H fhduq 0 isg 4095 f;la jk w;r" 1 msgqfõ w;:H fhduq 4096 isg 8191 f;la
          wdÈ jYfhka fõ.

     •    The Present/absent bit indicates the validity of the entry. If this bit is 1, the entry is valid and can
          be used. If it is 0, then the relevant virtual page is not in physical memory.

          Ndú;hg .; yels h' ìgqj 0 kï wod< w;:H msgqj fN!;sl u;lfha ke;.

    Page Size           = 4KB
    Page size in bytes = 22×210
                        = 212
    No. of offset bits = 12

    Page Availability
    msgq ,nd .ekSfï yelshdj

    •   The virtual address 0001 0000 0000 0011 corresponds to: Page number: 1
        w;:H ,smskh 0001 0000 0000 0011 wkqrEm fõ( msgq wxlh( 1
    •   From the page table, we can see that the entry for page number 1 has the Present/Absent bit set to 1,
        indicating that the page is present in physical memory.
        msgq j.=fjka" msgq wxl ^1 i|yd jk m%fõYfha Present/absent bit 1 f,i ilid we;s nj" msgqj
        fN!;sl u;lfha mj;sk nj fmkakqï lrk nj wmg oel.; yel'

    Physical Address Mapping
    fN!;sl ,smsk is;shï.; lsÍu

    •   The frame number for page 1 is 101 (binary)
        1 msgqj i|yd rduq wxlh 101 ^oaúuh&
    •   The page size is 4 KB, so the offset bits (last 12 bits) of the virtual address will remain the same in
        the physical address.
        msgq m%udKh 4 KB fõ" tneúka w;:H ,smskfha ´*afiÜ ìgq ^wjika ìgq 12& fN!;sl ,smskfhys
        tf,iu mj;skq we;'
    •   Therefore when mapping the virtual address to the physical addres we only change the number of bits
        used to represent the page number.
        tneúka fN!;sl ,smsk fj; w;:H ,smskh is;shï.; lsÍfï§ wms fjkia lrkafka msgq wxlh
        ksfhdackh lsÍug Ndú;d lrk ìgq .Kk muKs'
    •   Therefore, the 15-bit physical address that the virtual address 0001 0000 0000 0011 will be mapped

        tneúka" 0001 0000 0000 0011 hk w;:H ,smskh is;shï.; flfrk ìgq 15 fN!;sl ,smskh
        jkafka( 101 0000 0000 0011

 (d) Give two reasons as to why a page of a process may not be in physical memory.
     l%shdhkhl msgqjla (page) fN!;sl u;lfha fkdue;s ùug fya;= folla imhkak'

    Insufficient Physical Memory
    fN!;sl u;lh m%udKj;a fkdùu

    •   If the total physical memory (RAM) available on the system is not sufficient to hold all the pages
        required by the running processes, the operating system will have to swap out some pages to secondary
        storage (e.g. hard disk) to make room for new pages.
        moaO;sfha mj;sk iïmQ¾K fN!;sl u;lh RAM Odjk l%shdj,Skag wjYH ish¨u msgq r|jd ;nd
        .ekSug m%udKj;a fkdfõ kï" kj msgq i|yd bv ie,iSu i|yd fufyhqï moaO;shg iuyr msgq
        oaú;shsl .nvdjg ^Wod( oDV ;eáh& udre lsÍug isÿfõ'

    •   In this case, the pages that are swapped out to disk will not be present in physical memory, and
        accessing them will result in a page fault.
        fuu wjia:dfjys§" ;eáh fj; udre lrk ,o msgq fN!;sl u;lfha fkdmj;skq we;s w;r" tAjdg
        msúiSfuka msgq fodaIhla we;s fõ'

    Page Replacement
    msgq m%;sia:dmkh

    •   Even if there is sufficient physical memory, the operating system may still need to swap out some pages
        to make room for new ones.
        m%udKj;a fN!;sl u;lhla ;snqKo" kj msgq i|yd bv ie,iSug fufyhqï moaO;shg iuyr msgq
        udre lsÍug wjYH úh yelsh'

    •   This is because the operating system uses page replacement algorithms to decide which pages to evict
        from physical memory when new pages need to be brought in.
        kj msgq f.k tAug wjYH jQ úg fN!;sl u;lfhka bj;a l< hq;= msgq ;SrKh lsÍug fufyhqï
        moaO;sh msgq m%;sia:dmk we,af.dß;u Ndú;d lrk neúks'

    •   The pages that are selected for replacement and swapped out to disk will not be present in physical
        memory, causing page faults when accessed.
        m%;sia:dmkh i|yd f;dardf.k we;s iy ;eáhg udre lrk ,o msgq fN!;sl u;lfha fkdmj;shs"
        m%fõY jQ úg msgq fodaI we;s lrhs'

    This can happen even if the overall memory usage is well below the total physical memory available, due
    to the dynamic nature of memory allocation and the need for efficient page management by the operating
    system.
    u;lh fjkalsÍfï .;sl iajNdjh iy fufyhqï moaO;shg ld¾hlaIu msgq l<ukdlrKhl wjYH;djh
    fya;=fjka iuia; u;l Ndú;h mj;sk iïmQ¾K fN!;sl u;lhg jvd fnfyúka wvq jqjo fuh isÿúh
    yel'

 (e) Explain how the operating system could find the blocks of a file when the files are stored on a hard
     disk using
     oDV ;eáhl f.dkq .nvd lsÍu my; l%u Ndú;fhka isÿj we;s úg" fufyhqï moaO;sh f.dkqjl ldKav
     (blocks) fidhd.kakd wdldrh fjk fjku meyeÈ,s lrkak'

         (i) hdno (contiguous) úNckh
         (ii) wkql%ñl (index) úNckh

     (Hint: the use of the directory entry) (bÕsh: kdudj,s f;dr;=f¾ [directory entry] Ndú;h)

      Contiguous Allocation hdno úNckh

     In contiguous allocation, each file occupies a contiguous set of blocks on the disk. The directory entry
     contains the address of the starting block and the length of the allocated portion.
     tl È.g fjka lsÍfï l%ufha§" iEu f.dkqjlau ;eáfha tl È.g ldKav lÜg,hla .kS' äfrlagß
     m%fõYh wdrïNl fldgfiys ,smskh iy fjka l< fldgfiys È. wvx.= fõ'

     •     Directory Entry: The directory entry contains the address of the starting block and the length of the
           allocated portion.
           kdudj,s m%fõYh( kdudj,s m%fõYfha wdrïNl fldgfiys ,smskh iy fjka lr we;s fldgfiys
           È. wvx.= fõ'

     •     Starting Block Address: The starting block address is used to locate the first block of the file.
           wdrïNl jdrK ,smskh( f.dkqfõ m<uq fldgi fidhd .ekSug wdrïNl jdrK ,smskh Ndú;d lrhs

     •     Length of Allocated Portion: The length of the allocated portion is used to determine the number of
           blocks occupied by the file.
           fjka l< fldgfiys È.( f.dkqj úiska w,a,df.k we;s ldKav .Kk ;SrKh lsÍu i|yd fjka
           lrk ,o fldgfiys È. Ndú;d fõ'

     •     Block Sequence: The starting block address and the length of the allocated portion are used to
           determine the sequence of blocks occupied by the file.
           jdrK wkqms<sfj,( f.dkqj úiska w,a,df.k we;s ldKav wkqms<sfj, ;SrKh lsÍu i|yd wdrïNl
           jdrK ,smskh iy fjka l< fldgfiys È. Ndú;d fõ'

     For example, if a file 'mail' starts from block 19 with a length of 6 blocks, the directory entry would
     contain:
     WodyrKhla f,i" ;eme,a f.dkqjla ldKav 19 isg ldKav 6 l È.lska wdrïN jkafka kï" ny¨u
     we;=<;a fõ

     Starting Block Address: 19
     wdrïNl ldKav ,smskh( 19

     Length of Allocated Portion: 6
     fjka l< fldgfiys È.( 6

     Using this information, the operating system can locate the blocks of the file by simply adding the
     starting block address to the block number. For example, to access the 3rd block of the file, the operating
     system would add 3 to the starting block address (19 + 3 = 22).
     fuu f;dr;=re Ndú;d lrñka" fufyhqï moaO;shg ldKav wxlhg wdrïNl ldKav ,smskh tl;=
     lsÍfuka f.dkqfõ ldKav fidhd .; yel' WodyrKhla f,i" f.dkqfõ 3 jk fldgi fj; m%fõY ùu
     i|yd" fufyhqï moaO;sh wdrïNl íf,dla ,smskhg 3 tl;= lrhs 19 + 3 } 22.

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                 -44-                                           #ictfromabc

     Index Allocation

     In index allocation, each file has an index block that contains pointers to the blocks occupied by the file.
     The directory entry contains the address of the index block.
     o¾Yl fjkalsÍfï§" iEu f.dkqjlu o¾Yl ldKavhla we;" th f.dkqj úiska w,a,df.k we;s ldKav
     fj; o¾Yl wvx.= fõ' kdudj,s m%fõYfha o¾Yl ldKavhl ,smskh wvx.= fõ'

     •   Directory Entry: The directory entry contains the address of the index block.
         kdudj,s m%fõYh( kdudj,s m%fõYfha o¾Yl fldgfia ,smskh wvx.= fõ'

     •   Index Block: The index block contains pointers to the blocks occupied by the file
         Index ldKav( Index ldKav tfla f.dkqj úiska w,a,df.k we;s ldKav i|yd fmdhskag¾ wvx.= fõ'

     •   Block Pointers: The index block contains pointers to the blocks occupied by the file.
         ldKav fmdhskag¾( bkafvlaia ldKavhl f.dkqj w,a,df.k isák ldKav j,g fmdhskag¾ wvx.=
         fõ'

     For example, if a file 'text1' has an index block at block 19, the directory entry would contain:
     WodyrKhla f,i" text f.dkqjl ^19& jeks fldgfiys o¾Yl ldKavhla ;sfí kï" kdudj,s m%fõYfha
     wvx.= jkafka(

     Index Block Address: 19
     o¾Yl ldKav ,smskh( 19

     Using this information, the operating system can locate the blocks of the file by following the pointers in
     the index block. For example, to access the 3rd block of the file, the operating system would follow the
     pointers in the index block to locate the 3rd block.
     fuu f;dr;=re Ndú;d lrñka" fufyhqï moaO;shg o¾Yl ldKavfha we;s fmdhskag¾ wkq.ukh
     lsÍfuka f.dkqfõ fldgia fidhd .; yel' WodyrKhla f,i" f.dkqfõ 3 jk fldgi fj; m%fõY ùu
     i|yd" fufyhqï moaO;sh 3 jk fldgi fidhd .ekSug o¾Yl fldgfia we;s fmdhskag¾ wkq.ukh
     lrhs'

     In both contiguous allocation and index allocation, the directory entry plays a crucial role in locating the
     blocks of a file on a hard disk. The operating system uses the directory entry to determine the starting
     block address, length of the allocated portion, or index block address, which is then used to locate the
     blocks of the file.
     tl È.g fjka lsÍu iy o¾Yl fjka lsÍu hk foflysu" oDV ;eáhl f.dkqjl fldgia ia:dk.;
     lsÍfï§ kdudj,s m%fõYh ;SrKd;aul ld¾hNdrhla bgq lrhs' fufyhqï moaO;sh wdrïNl jdrK
     ,smskh" fjka lrk ,o fldgfiys È. fyda o¾Yl ldKav ,smskh ;SrKh lsÍu i|yd kdudj,s
     m%fõYh Ndú;d lrhs" th f.dkqfõ ldKav fidhd .ekSug Ndú;d lrhs'
